# 2603.06478v1_Can_deleterious_mutations_surf_deterministic_population_waves__A_functional_law_

## Page 1


Can deleterious mutations surf deterministic population waves?
A functional law of large numbers for
a spatial model of Muller’s ratchet
João Luiz de Oliveira Madeira∗
Marcel Ortgiese†
Sarah Penington†
March 9, 2026
Abstract
The spatial Muller’s ratchet is a model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge [34]
to study the impact of cooperation and competition on the fitness of an expanding asexual
population. The model is an interacting particle system consisting of particles performing
symmetric random walks that reproduce and die with rates that depend on the local number
of particles. For each particle, we keep track of the number of deleterious mutations that
it carries, and after each birth event, with some positive probability, the offspring particle
can acquire an additional mutation that gives it a lower reproduction rate than its parent.
We show that under an appropriate scaling, the process converges weakly to the solution of
an infinite system of partial differential equations (PDEs), confirming non-rigorous compu-
tations of Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in [34]. In the PDE limit, when the reaction term of
the system of PDEs is monostable, we establish bounds on the ratio between the density of
particles with a given number of mutations and the density of particles without mutations.
If the reaction term satisfies a Fisher-KPP condition, we can also rigorously determine the
spreading speed of the population into an empty habitat. Finally, by considering the PDE
limit of a form of tracer dynamics, we answer the question of whether deleterious mutations
can surf population waves in this setting.
Keywords: Muller’s ratchet; spatial birth-death processes; hydrodynamic limits; non-local partial dif-
ferential equations; gene surfing.
1
Introduction
In this article, we rigorously establish the scaling limit of a spatial reaction-diffusion system of
interacting particles with countably infinitely many mutation types. The system is a generali-
sation of a model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in [34] to investigate the impact
of cooperation and competition on the propagation of deleterious mutations through an asex-
ual population expanding in space, where individual birth and death rates depend on the local
population density. Before formally defining the interacting particle system in Section 2, in
this section we outline the biological motivation for the model, as well as the (non-rigorous)
derivations in [34] that we prove in this article.
1.1
Motivation
As a central mechanism of evolution, when an individual passes its genetic material to its
offspring, a mutation may occur and the genetic material is modified. This modification may
have an effect on the fitness of the individual to survive and reproduce: a mutation may be
∗Department of Statistics, University of Oxford, UK
†Department of Mathematical Sciences, University of Bath, UK
1
arXiv:2603.06478v1  [math.PR]  6 Mar 2026


## Page 2


deleterious (reducing fitness), advantageous (increasing fitness), or neutral (having no effect on
fitness).
Although fitness is a key factor in determining the survival probability of an individual, in a
spatially structured population this probability also depends on the local population density [34].
Higher density may either decrease survival probability due to competition or increase it due to
cooperation.
It is known that rare neutral mutations can propagate across large regions during range
expansion, a phenomenon called gene surfing, first described by Edmonds et al. [26]. Roughly
speaking, if competition dominates over cooperation and so the per-capita growth rate is high
at low population densities, then a neutral mutation appearing at the front of an expanding
population (i.e. at the leading edge of the expanding population) can spread locally with high
probability, since the population density at the front is usually low. Since colonisation of new
habitat is driven by individuals near the front, such mutations can become prevalent over large
spatial regions [34].
Gene surfing of neutral mutations has been studied through simulations of discrete particle
systems, partial differential equations (PDEs), and stochastic PDEs (see [26, 39, 70]), and rig-
orously in PDE-based models [35, 70]. We also refer the reader to [70] for a discussion on the
possible different definitions of gene surfing of neutral mutations. In contrast, the gene surfing
of deleterious mutations has received less attention.
To address the question of whether deleterious mutations can also surf, Foutel-Rodier and
Etheridge introduced the spatial Muller’s ratchet [34], a spatial extension of a mechanism first
proposed by Muller [61] to explain the evolution of recombination and sexual reproduction. In
asexual reproduction, chromosomes are inherited as indivisible units, so the number of mutations
along an ancestral lineage can only increase.
Since the majority of mutations in biological
systems are deleterious [6], this leads to a gradual decline in overall population fitness [27]. In
Muller’s ratchet models, when all individuals in the most-adapted class in the population acquire
at least one additional deleterious mutation, the minimum mutational load in the population
increases — this is known as a ‘click’ of the ratchet. Such models may help us to understand
the accumulation of deleterious mutations in microorganisms, in mitochondrial DNA, and in
the Y chromosome in mammals (see e.g. [2, 42, 46] and references therein). This mechanism
may also explain the evolutionary advantage of sexual reproduction, where recombination may
reduce mutation load along lineages, thereby preventing further clicks of the ratchet. From a
mathematical perspective, Muller’s ratchet has been extensively studied in populations without
spatial structure [27,36,38,57,66].
The model in [34] shares the same key feature of deleterious mutations, but also includes a
spatial component and density dependent birth and death rates. We now describe the model.

The change in the expression for the spreading speed at B =
2
1−µ reflects a transition from pulled
to pushed expansion waves [35,70]. Heuristically, in the case of pulled waves, the critical speed
is the same as for the system of PDEs linearised around the state 0, i.e. the wave is pulled by
its leading edge. On the other hand, in pushed waves, the critical speed is determined not only
by the leading edge, but by the whole front, i.e. the wave is pushed from behind [35]. Pulled
expansion waves are observed when the highest per-capita growth rate is achieved at very low
population density, i.e. in particular when the population dynamics is of Fisher-KPP type [70].
On the other hand, when the per-capita growth rate is negative at very low population densities,
i.e. when the dynamics displays a strong Allee effect, pushed expansion waves are observed [70].
When the maximum growth rate is achieved at an intermediate population density, but the
population still exhibits growth even at low population densities, then the dynamics shows a
weak Allee effect; in this case, the wave can be either pulled or pushed, depending on the model
parameters. From (1.2), Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge conclude that for B ∈[0, 2/(1 −µ)), the
dynamics of the system of PDEs (1.1) exhibits pulled behaviour, while for B > 2/(1 −µ), the
system exhibits pushed behaviour.
For an expanding population, in the PDE limit scaling, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge also
state that, under a weak selection–low mutation regime, i.e. assuming that s, µ ≪1, the sta-
tionary solution ˆu = (ˆu)k∈N0 of the system of PDEs (1.1) with ˆu0 ̸= 0 satisfies

From a mathematical perspective, there are several challenges in rigorously proving the
conjectures of Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in [34]. First, since we allow for the existence of
particles carrying an arbitrarily large number of mutations, we must keep track of infinitely
many types of particles. Moreover, there are no a priori bounds on the number of particles per
deme, and both birth and death rates are unbounded. Furthermore, the fact that particles at
the same deme with different number of mutations interact with each other leads to ‘non-local
interactions’ in both the interacting particle system and the system of PDEs (1.1).
In fact, it is highly non-trivial to show that the particle systems even exists (when started
with an infinite number of particles), not only for the reasons mentioned above, but also be-
cause the system turns out to be non-monotone. We address this question in our companion
3


## Page 4


article [56], where we construct a general version of the interacting particle system introduced
in [34] rigorously. Moreover, we prove moment bounds that will also be essential in this article.
In this article, we use the results in [56] and rigorously derive the scaling limit of the general
spatial Muller’s ratchet model, and we confirm several of the conjectures of [34]. In particular,
we prove convergence to the limiting PDE system under appropriate scaling assumptions, derive
the spreading speed in the Fisher-KPP regime (that is, for B ≤1/(1 −µ)), and establish an
evolution equation for the mutational profile that agrees with (1.3) in the weak selection–low
mutation regime. Finally, we also address the question of gene surfing via a construction of
tracer dynamics.
Structure of the article. In Section 2, we define the spatial Muller’s ratchet model, and
in Sections 2.1–2.4 we state the main results of this article. In Section 2.5, we briefly review
some techniques commonly used to establish hydrodynamic limit results for interacting particle
systems, and explain why they are not directly applicable to our setting. In Section 2.6, we
informally present the key ideas behind the proofs of our results. In Section 3, we review some
concepts that will be used in the article, including the notion of solutions to the (infinite) system
of PDEs. The main results of our companion article [56] that will be used in this article are
stated in Section 3.1.
The proofs of our results can be found in Sections 4–8.
In Section 4, we use a Green’s
function representation of the random walk semigroup to establish regularity properties of the
population density. We use these properties to derive tightness in the space of sequences of
Radon measures in Section 5. Tightness of the process in a suitable generalisation of the usual
Lp space is established in Section 6. Regularity and uniqueness properties of limiting solutions
to a system of PDEs are established in Section 7. In Section 8, we use a Feynman-Kac formula
to determine the asymptotic behaviour of the limiting system of PDEs. Appendix A contains
standard results and technical estimates used in Sections 4–8.

Model definition and main results
Let N ∈N be a scaling parameter and LN > 0 a space renormalisation parameter depending on
N. The model consists of particles moving on the rescaled one-dimensional lattice L−1
N Z, where
we call each point x ∈L−1
N Z a deme. Each particle (representing an individual in the population
that carries a unique chromosome) is characterised by two features: the number of deleterious
mutations that it carries and its spatial location. For each t ≥0, x ∈L−1
N Z and k ∈N0, let
ηN
k (t, x) denote the number of particles at deme x carrying exactly k mutations at time t. Note

k (t, x))k∈N0. The total number of particles living at deme x at time
t is given by ∥ηN(t, x)∥ℓ1 = P∞

k (t, x).
Let mN > 0 be the migration rate, and let (sk)k∈N0 be a sequence of fitness parameters,
where sk ≥0 denotes the fitness of a particle carrying k mutations. Let q+, q−: R+ →R+ be
non-negative functions, and let µ ∈[0, 1] be the mutation probability. We will precisely state
our assumptions on mN, (sk)k∈N0, q+ and q−later in this section. The spatial Muller’s ratchet
process (ηN(t))t≥0 = (ηN
k (t, x) : k ∈N0, x ∈L−1
N Z)t≥0 can be described informally as follows:
• Migration events: For each t ≥0 and x ∈L−1
N Z, each particle living at deme x at time t
independently jumps at rate mN to a uniformly chosen deme from {x −L−1
N , x + L−1
N }.
• Reproduction events: For each t ≥0, x ∈L−1
N Z and k ∈N0, each particle carrying k
mutations at deme x at time t reproduces independently at rate skq+(∥ηN(t−, x)∥ℓ1/N).
After such an event, a new offspring particle will be added at deme x. With probability
1 −µ, the offspring particle will carry k mutations, and with probability µ it will carry
k + 1 mutations. Note that in both cases, the parent particle remains alive and keeps the
same number of mutations.
• Death events: For each t ≥0 and x ∈L−1
N Z, each particle at deme x at time t dies
independently at rate q−(∥ηN(t−, x)∥ℓ1/N), regardless of its number of mutations. When
a particle dies, it is simply removed from the process.

and the space renormalisation parameter LN will satisfy the following assumptions.

Now, since in our model we do not impose a priori bounds on the number of particles per
deme, we make the following assumptions on the birth and death rates.
Assumption 3 (Birth and death polynomial rates). Suppose that q+, q−: [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) are
polynomials such that 0 ≤deg q+ < deg q−.
By Assumption 3, the leading coefficient of the polynomial q+ is non-negative, and the
leading coefficient of q−is strictly positive. Note also that the scaling parameter N can be
thought of as being proportional to the local carrying capacity of the population. Indeed, since
deg q+ < deg q−, when the number of particles at a deme is much larger than N, then death
events happen at a higher rate than birth events, providing a local regulation mechanism for
the population density.

2.2
The asymptotic behaviour of the limiting process
In [34], Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge state (without rigorous proofs) that for the spatial Muller’s
ratchet model, if the initial proportions of individuals carrying each number of mutations in a
population spreading into an empty habitat were the same proportions as an equilibrium point of
the system of PDEs (2.11) (i.e. if the population were in selection–mutation equilibrium), then
9


## Page 10


the proportions of individuals carrying each number of mutations would subsequently be the
same in the bulk as in the front of the range expansion. Using this hypothesis, they determine
(non-rigorusly) the spreading speed of the population into an empty habitat.
Our next main results establish rigorous versions of these conjectures under certain conditions
on the reaction term (2.12) of the system of PDEs (2.11). To introduce these conditions, we
first recall the terminology usually used to describe one-dimensional reaction-diffusion equations.
Consider the PDE
∂tU = m
2 △U + g(U)
for t > 0,

0 g(U) dU > 0. We say that the reaction term g
is monostable if g′(0) > 0, g′(1) < 0 and g(x) > 0 for all x ∈(0, 1) (see e.g. [35]). We say
that the reaction term g is of Fisher-KPP type if g is monostable and g(U) ≤g′(0)U for all
U ∈(0, 1). From a population dynamics point of view, the Fisher-KPP condition corresponds to
the setting in which competition dominates over cooperation. In our next definition, we adapt
these concepts to the system of PDEs (2.11).
Definition 2.3 (Monostable and Fisher-KPP reaction terms). We say that the reaction term
F = (Fk)k∈N0 : ℓ+
1 →ℓ1 defined in (2.12) is monostable if the sequence of fitness parameters
(sk)k∈N0 satisfies Assumption 2 and is strictly decreasing, the mutation rate satisfies µ ∈(0, 1),
and the functions q+, q−: [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) satisfy Assumption 3 and the following conditions:
(i) q+(U) ≥q−(U)
∀U ∈[0, 1].

(v) (1 −µ)q+(0) −q−(0) > 0.
Finally, if additionally to the conditions above, we also have
(1 −µ)q+(U) −q−(U) ≤(1 −µ)q+(0) −q−(0)
∀U ∈[0, 1],
(2.14)
then we say that the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 is of Fisher-KPP type.
Note that if the reaction term g(U) = U(q+(U) −q−(U)) of the one-dimensional PDE
∂tU = m

(2.15)

Moreover, if the reaction term of (2.15) is monostable, then q+(0) > q−(0), and therefore
condition (v) of Definition 2.3 must hold for sufficiently small µ. We also must have q+(U) > 0
for all U ∈(0, 1), but the additional condition q+(1) > 0 in condition (ii) is not implied by the
monostability of the reaction term of (2.15); this is a technical assumption required in our proofs.
This condition, however, is not restrictive, since it is reasonable to assume in biological models
that the per-capita reproduction and death rates are both strictly positive in high population
density.
In order to determine the evolution of the ratios between the proportions of particles carry-
ing a particular number of mutations, we will need some control on these ratios in the initial
condition. This will be our next assumption. Recall that we denote the Lebesgue measure on R

2.3
Application to different settings of population dynamics
In this subsection, we show how our results apply to different models of population growth.
Since our definition of the spatial Muller’s ratchet ηN is rather general, it can be used to model
asexual populations under different biological assumptions. For instance, taking
q+(U) := r(BU + 1)
and
q−(U) := r(BU + 1)U
∀U ≥0, for some r > 0 and B ≥0,
(2.19)
we recover the same birth and death rates as in the model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and
Etheridge in [34] (recall that we described this model in Section 1.1). Phenomenologically, r
indicates the Malthusian growth factor, and B reflects the dependence of the growth rate on the
local population density. Note that the value of the population density U at which the maximum
of the function
[0, ∞) ∋U 7→q+(U) −q−(U) = r(1 −U)(BU + 1)
is attained depends on the parameter B. If 0 ≤B ≤1, then the function attains its maximum
when U = 0, i.e. the maximum per-capita growth rate is attained at U = 0. We say that in this
case the population dynamics (neglecting, for now, the effect of deleterious mutations) satisfies a
Fisher-KPP condition [35]. Biologically, this means that competition prevails over cooperation
in determining the population dynamics.
For B > 1, the function [0, ∞) ∋U 7→q+(U) −q−(U) attains its maximum at U = B−1
2B > 0.
In this case, we say the population (neglecting the effect of deleterious mutations) exhibits a weak
Allee effect, since the maximum per-capita growth rate is attained at an intermediate population
density rather than at low population density [34]. From a biological point of view, this scenario
corresponds to the case where both competition and cooperation affect the population dynamics.
Our Theorem 2.1 shows that for q+ and q−given by (2.19), under Assumptions 1 and 2
on (mN)N∈N, (LN)N∈N and (sk)k∈N0 and suitable assumptions on the initial condition, the
spatial Muller’s ratchet ηN converges as N →∞to the solution of the corresponding system
of PDEs (2.11) (which is the same as (1.1) in the special case sk = (1 −s)k for all k ∈N0).
Since the rates q+ and q−satisfy Definition 2.3(i)-(iv) for any B ≥0, r > 0 and µ ∈(0, 1),
we have from Theorem 2.5 quantitative bounds on the proportions of the population carrying
k ∈N0 mutations in the solution of the system of PDEs (2.11) at large times, for suitable initial
conditions. The assumptions for Theorem 2.7, which determines the spreading speed, require
that the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 defined in (2.12) is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of
Definition 2.3, i.e. that for all U ∈[0, 1],
(1 −µ)q+(U) −q−(U) = r(1 −µ −U)(BU + 1) ≤(1 −µ)q+(0) −q−(0) = r(1 −µ).

Then the function U 7→q+(U) −q−(U) = (1 −U)(U −B) is negative for U ∈[0, B). We
say that the population (neglecting the effect of deleterious mutations) exhibits a strong Allee
effect [35]. Biologically, this means that cooperation is fundamental for population growth, as
the population shrinks at low population densities. In this case, our Theorem 2.1 again shows
the convergence of the spatial Muller’s ratchet to the solution of the corresponding system
of PDEs (2.11). However, the asymptotic behaviour of solutions of (2.11) in this case is not
determined by Theorems 2.5 and 2.7, because the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 given by (2.12)
is not monostable in the sense of Definition 2.3. Determining the asymptotic behaviour in this
case is an interesting open problem.
We can also consider the effect of different choices of fitness parameters (sk)k∈N0. We first
consider the model introduced in [34], where the sequence of fitness parameters is given by
sk := (1 −s)k ∀k ∈N0, for some fixed s ∈(0, 1). In this case, when a new mutation is acquired,
the fitness of a chromosome always decreases by a factor (1−s). In other words, the effect of each
mutation is independent of the genetic background. Biologically, we say there is no epistasis. As
explained in Section 1.1, in the case s, µ ≪1, i.e. under a weak selection–low mutation regime,
Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge derive non-rigorously that the stationary solution of the system of

which agrees with the right-hand side of (1.3). In other words, roughly speaking, in the weak
selection–low mutation regime, our Theorem 2.5 shows that the approximate stationary solution
given in [34] governs the proportions of the population carrying different numbers of mutations
at large times in solutions of (2.11).
Our Assumption 2 also allows for other choices of fitness parameters. For instance, one could
take sk :=
1
k+1 ∀k ∈N0. In this case, the addition of another mutation to a genotype with k
mutations leads to a reduction of fitness by a factor sk+1/sk = k/(k + 1), which depends on the
previous number of mutations k. In biological terms, we say that the model allows some degree
of epistasis, which can be important in some biological applications [37].
2.4
Tracer dynamics and gene surfing
Our final result answers the question that motivated this article: can deleterious mutations surf
population waves? Although we are not yet able to approach this question in the stochastic
setting, we can answer it when we restrict ourselves to deterministic waves, i.e. when consid-
ering the limiting system of PDEs (2.11). We will use the idea of tracer dynamics introduced
by Hallateschek and Nelson in [39] and formalised by Garnier and co-authors in [35] in the
deterministic setting.
Roughly speaking, in the particle system setting, the idea of tracer dynamics is to label some
subset of particles at time 0. The label does not affect the mutation probability or the migration,
reproduction and death rates. When a labelled particle reproduces, its offspring particle is also
a labelled particle. The aim is to track the number of labelled particles at each deme over time,
and use this to infer information about the typical history of particles in the system. In order to
translate this idea to the context of the system of PDEs (2.11), we take f∗= (f∗

We will recall the definition of continuous mild solutions to the system of PDEs (2.25) in

the monostable case). It shows that for a population expanding according to (2.11), although
(by Theorem 2.5) there is a positive fraction of the population carrying deleterious mutations
in both the front and the bulk of the expanding population, this fraction descends from the
expanding subpopulation without mutations and is not the result of surfing of previously existing
deleterious mutations; see Figure 2 for a simulation of this phenomenon. Therefore, we can say
that deleterious mutations cannot surf deterministic population waves.
2.5
Related work
The study of the convergence of interacting particle systems to reaction–diffusion partial differ-
ential equations has been a very active area of research in probability theory since at least the
1980s. For spatial birth–death processes, there are several approaches to proving convergence
to a hydrodynamic limit, depending on the assumptions on the initial condition, the spatial
domain, and the dynamics of the discrete model. In this subsection, we briefly review some of
16


## Page 17

time the snapshot is taken is denoted by T. Colours indicate the density of population carrying

the T = 0 figure. Shaded regions indicate the labelled fraction of the population. At time

labelled population, descended from the initial mutant subpopulation, does not propagate with

this literature and explain why the methods developed previously cannot be directly applied to
the proof of Theorem 2.1.
Roughly speaking, the literature on hydrodynamic scaling limits of spatial birth–death pro-
cesses can be divided into five main categories:
(A) Works that analyse models in which there is an a priori bound on the local number of
particles, so that in the limit the population density is always uniformly bounded (see for
instance [24,25,60]).
(B) Works that assume the spatial domain of the process is compact, e.g. the torus Td := [0, 1]d
(see for instance [3,4,11–14,31,50,51]).
(C) Works that apply the method of correlation functions to derive the limit [15,21,65,71].
(D) Works that use the relative entropy method [45,59,65].
(E) Works that analyse models with finitely many particles in the whole domain and bounded
reproduction rates, so that there are bounds on the moments of the total mass [28,32].
To the best of our knowledge, there are no existing works that address spatial birth–death
processes with infinitely many particle types, unbounded reproduction rates, and an unbounded
number of particles per site simultaneously. Consequently, the techniques developed in the works
mentioned above cannot be applied to our model without significant adaptation.
Works in category (A) typically construct the birth–death interacting particle system using
a countable collection of Poisson processes. Since, in these works, the number of particles per
deme is bounded by some integer N, the approximate population density is uniformly bounded,
see e.g. [24,25,60]. This uniform bound means that convergence can be studied in the space of
continuous functions, which is strictly stronger than convergence in the space of Radon measures.
Since no uniform density bound holds in our setting, these techniques cannot be applied directly.
To handle the absence of uniform bounds, Arnold and Theodosopulu [3, 4], Blount [11–
14], and Kotelenez [50, 51] developed techniques in the 1980s and 1990s that apply to spatial
birth–death processes on the one-dimensional torus T = [0, 1]. These works (category (B) in
the list above) exploit the construction of an explicit orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space
L2([0, 1]) to analyse weak convergence.
Importantly, their techniques rely on properties of
martingales taking values in Hilbert spaces, that are not shared by martingales taking values
17


## Page 18


in general Banach spaces. Since our model involves infinitely many particle types, the solution
of the limiting system of PDEs (2.11) is most naturally understood as an ℓ1-valued function on
[0, ∞) × R. Consequently, it is not natural to study convergence in Hilbert spaces, and these
techniques do not transfer directly to our setting.
In category (C), Boldrighini et al. were the first to rigorously analyse a spatial birth–death
process with polynomial birth and death rates on the whole real line [15, 21]. They consider
a single-type particle system on the lattice εZ, where ε > 0 is a spatial scaling parameter,
and study its behaviour as ε →0.
Importantly, their setting does not involve scaling the
initial number of particles per deme by 1/ε to produce a mean-field limit. Instead, they use
correlation functions, a form of duality function, to bound local moments and show that the limit
satisfies a so-called BBGKY hierarchy consistent with the target PDE. More recently, Tendron
also used the BBGKY hierarchy to study the scaling limit of branching processes with local
competition [71].
The BBGKY hierarchy becomes intractable for systems with infinitely many particle types
(see [15, Section 4]), which means this method is not feasible for our purposes. Nonetheless,
we adapt some ideas from [15, 21], in particular the use of correlation functions, to obtain
the estimates in our companion article [56, Theorem 2.3] that are used in the proof of the
hydrodynamic limit result in this article.
The relative entropy method, used by Mourragui [59] and others [45, 65] (category (D)),
requires the solution of the limiting PDE to be uniformly bounded away from zero. In our
setting, the limiting population density u(t, x) takes values in ℓ1, so for any t ≥0 and x ∈R we
have limk→∞uk(t, x) = 0. It is therefore not clear how to apply the relative entropy method to
our model.
In a recent article closely related to our work, Etheridge et al. [28] study a spatial birth–death
process on Rd with non-local interactions. The authors prove weak convergence of the process
under different scaling regimes, either retaining the non-local interaction term in the limit or
not. A notable advantage of the approach in [28] is that it does not require a priori bounds
on the density of particles per unit region. By assuming the reproduction rate is uniformly
bounded and that the initial configuration contains only finitely many particles, the authors
derive precise moment estimates for the total mass of the process, which they use to establish
tightness of the sequence of interacting particle systems and to characterise the limit. Moreover,
to prove uniqueness of the limiting PDE in the space of measures, they apply [53, Theorem 3.5],
which requires the reproduction and death rates to be uniformly bounded and globally Lipschitz
continuous.
In a similar vein, Flandoli and Huang [32] analyse the scaling limit of Brownian particles
in Rd with a uniformly bounded reproduction rate, proving convergence to the Fisher–KPP
equation without non-local interaction. Their technique also relies on the uniform bound on
the reproduction rate and on having a finite initial number of particles. In contrast, in our
setting, in general the reproduction rate is not uniformly bounded, and neither the reproduction
nor death rates are globally Lipschitz continuous. We also allow for infinitely many particles
at time 0. Consequently, we cannot rely on these methods to prove convergence of the spatial
Muller’s ratchet to the solution of the system of PDEs (2.11) without substantial adaptations.
We would also like to highlight some previous work on the hydrodynamic limit of coagulation-
fragmentation processes [40,41,54]. Although in these works the authors study particle systems
with infinitely many types, there is no creation of mass, since only coagulation, fragmentation
and diffusion events are allowed. Hence, the total mass is always uniformly bounded, and L∞
bounds can be obtained on the population densities. This is not possible in our setting, which
makes our proof significantly different.
Finally, there is also previous work on the scaling limits of interacting particle systems with
infinitely many types of particles but without spatial dependence [7–9, 68, 73]. These systems
arise naturally from questions involving graph dynamics and epidemic models. In this class
18


## Page 19


of systems, it is often possible to describe the deterministic scaling limit in terms of ordinary
differential equations taking values in the space of sequences ℓ1. The fact that we analyse a
population with spatial structure, however, makes our approach very different.
2.6
Overview of the proofs
Before introducing the main ideas behind the proofs, we highlight the principal challenges that
must be overcome in the analysis of the spatial Muller’s ratchet model. First, the lack of a
priori bounds on the local number of particles — together with unbounded birth and death
rates — prevent us from establishing uniform estimates in space; in particular, we are not
able to bound E
h
∥uN(t, ·)∥L∞(R;ℓ1)
i
. Second, letting the ‘type’ of a particle denote the number
of mutations that it carries, having infinitely many types of particles requires us to embed the
stochastic process in non-standard Polish spaces to study its scaling limit. Finally, the dynamics
is non-local in type space, since particles of different types in the same deme interact with each
other (in the sense that birth and death rates depend on the total number of particles in the
deme).

A major challenge in the proof of Theorem 2.1 is that, since we embed our process in the
space of M (R)N0-valued càdlàg functions, tightness alone does not imply that any subsequential
limit has sample paths lying in the subset of M (R)N0 given by sequences of measures that are
absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, nor does it guarantee that the birth
and death rate polynomials of the local population density converge in the space of measures.
Moreover, even after establishing the existence of a sequence of densities for the limiting measure-
valued process, we note that, because tightness is proved in the space of M (R)N0-valued càdlàg
functions, proving uniqueness of the limit requires us to establish uniqueness of solutions to the
system of PDEs (2.11) in M (R)N0. This is highly non-trivial due to the non-linear reaction term
in (2.12) that is neither uniformly bounded nor globally Lipschitz continuous, and because, as
explained at the start of this subsection, we do not have any guarantee that the limiting solution
remains uniformly bounded in space over finite time intervals (see [19,20,53] and the references
therein for a discussion of the challenges in establishing uniqueness of measure-valued solutions
to non-linear PDEs).
To overcome these difficulties, we will also show tightness of (uN)N∈N in the functional space

Together with the uniqueness of mild solutions, this relation implies uniqueness for the measure-

It is important to note that although we work in the general setting of ℓ1-valued functions,
the framework extends naturally to the analysis of scaling limits of interacting particle systems
with finitely many particle types. In this case, condition (ii) in the tightness criterion is trivial.
Moreover, our method does not require the particle density to be almost surely bounded, nor
does it require the birth and death rates to be uniformly bounded, or the initial number of
particles to be finite.
Asymptotic properties of solutions of the PDE. The smoothness properties of the limiting
solution of the system of PDEs (2.11) will allow us to write the evolution of each of the densities
uk in terms of a Feynman-Kac formula. Then, in Section 8, using the definition of a monostable
reaction term from Definition 2.3 and an induction argument, we will prove Theorem 2.5. Armed
with this result, we will then be able to adapt an argument from [64] (which in turn is based
on ideas in [17]) to compute the spreading speed. In [64], the spreading speed of solutions U to
21


## Page 22


the non-local Fisher-KPP equation is determined; by separately considering the cases when the
population density U is low or high, the Feynman-Kac formula and Brownian motion estimates
imply a lower bound on the spreading speed. The argument in [64] relies on the fact that when
the density is low, i.e. for small values of U, the per-capita growth rate is large, while when the
density is high, i.e. for large values of U, then this means that U has already propagated to the
region.
In our setting, we consider the cases when the total population density ∥u∥ℓ1 is large or
small, and analyse the impact of this on the dynamics of u0. We assume that the reaction
term F = (Fk)k∈N0 is of Fisher-KPP type (in the sense of Definition 2.3). Then, if the density
∥u∥ℓ1 is small, the per-capita growth rate of u0 is large. On the other hand, if ∥u∥ℓ1 is large,
Theorem 2.5 implies that u0 is bounded away from 0. In Section 8, by combining these ideas
and those of [64], we will be able to prove Theorem 2.7.
3
Preliminaries
In this section, first, in Section 3.1 we will state the main results of the companion article [56]
that we will use in this article. Then, in Section 3.2, we define the meaning of weak and mild
ℓ1-valued solutions to partial differential equations.
3.1
Moment bound and martingale problem for the spatial Muller’s ratchet
In this subsection, we give an overview of the properties of the spatial Muller’s ratchet proved
in our companion article [56].
We start by stating some topological properties of the state
space SN, which can be found in [56, Proposition A.5].
Proposition 3.1. For each N ∈N, the metric space (SN, dSN ) defined in (2.3) is complete and
separable. Moreover, a subset K ⊂SN is relatively compact in the topology induced by dSN if
and only if all the following conditions are satisfied:

We now prepare to formally introduce the process (ηN(t))t≥0 that we call the spatial Muller’s
ratchet. For any fixed N ∈N, under Assumptions 2 and 3, for an initial configuration ηN
satisfying (2.2), the process (ηN(t))t≥0 with ηN(0) = ηN almost surely will be given by the weak
limit of a sequence of approximating Feller processes (ηN,n)n∈N. More precisely, let (λn)n∈N ⊂
(0, ∞) and (Kn)n∈N ⊆N denote increasing sequences such that
lim
n→∞λn = ∞
and
lim
n→∞Kn = ∞.
For each n ∈N, let ΛN,n := L−1
N Z ∩[−λn, λn]; the process (ηN,n(t))t≥0 will be an SN-valued
càdlàg Markov process. Informally, the process ηN,n can be described as follows: particles outside
the box ΛN,n are frozen. All particles living inside ΛN,n can migrate and die (at the rates in the
informal description of ηN at the start of Section 2), but particles on the boundary of ΛN,n can
only migrate to a deme inside ΛN,n. Finally, only particles that carry Kn or fewer mutations are
22


## Page 23


able to reproduce (again, at the rates in the informal description at the start of Section 2). Since
the number of demes in ΛN,n is finite, and only particles carrying at most Kn mutations can
reproduce, by ignoring frozen particles, the process (ηN,n(t))t≥0 can be thought of as a Markov
process with finitely many types. A formal definition of the process (ηN,n(t))t≥0 in terms of its
infinitesimal generator is given in the companion article [56, Section 2]. In [56, Theorem 2.2], we
show that for ηN satisfying (2.2), conditioning on ηN,n(0) = ηN for every n ∈N, the sequence of
processes ((ηN,n(t))t≥0)n∈N0 converges weakly as n →∞. More precisely, define a set of initial

We are now ready to properly define the meaning of a weak solution to the PDE (2.11).
We will be thinking of weak solutions in the sense of distributions, rather than in the sense of
elements of Sobolev spaces (see [43, Chapter 2] for the difference between these concepts).
Definition 3.4 (Weak solution). We say that a measurable function u ∈L1,loc([0, ∞) × R; ℓ1)
is a weak solution to the system of PDEs (2.11) if the following conditions are satisfied:
(i) u(0, x) = f(x) for λ-almost every x ∈R.
(ii) u(T, ·) ∈L1,loc(R; ℓ1) for all T ≥0.
(iii) F(u) : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ1 is an element of L1,loc([0, ∞) × R; ℓ1), where F = (Fk)k∈N0 is the
reaction term defined in (2.12).

Definition 3.5 (Mild solution). We say that a measurable function u : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ1 is a
(global) mild solution to the system of PDEs (2.11) if and only if the following conditions are

Remark 3.6. We collect here some observations regarding Definition 3.5.
(a) Although our definition of a mild solution is compatible with the integral formulations used
in the Lr-regularity literature (see e.g. [67, Chapter 5]), classical semigroup approaches to
deterministic PDEs typically require mild solutions to be continuous in time and to satisfy
the variation-of-constants formula (3.5) pointwise in time (see e.g. [63, Section 4.1]). In
contrast, we work with a weaker formulation in which (3.5) holds almost everywhere in time
and in space. This choice reflects the fact that our construction of solutions takes place in
Lr-based function spaces, where time and space continuity is not available a priori.
(b) Let T ′ > 0 be fixed. If a measurable function u : [0, T ′] × R →ℓ1 satisfies Definition 3.5(i)
and (ii) for λ-almost every (T, x) ∈[0, T ′] × R, we say u is a (local) mild solution to the
system of PDEs (2.11).
Our next result shows that, under mild additional assumptions, a mild solution is also a
weak solution.
Lemma 3.7. Let u : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ+
1 denote a measurable function. Suppose that u is a mild
solution to the system of PDEs (2.11) in the sense of Definition 3.5, and that it satisfies the
following conditions:
(i) u(0, x) = f(x) for λ-almost every x ∈R.
(ii) For all T > 0, u

[0,T]×R denotes the restriction of u
to [0, T] × R.
(iii) For all T > 0, u satisfies identity (3.5) for λ-almost every x ∈R.
Then u is a weak solution to the system of PDEs (2.11) in the sense of Definition 3.4.
Although the equivalence between mild and weak solutions is classical under higher regularity
assumptions, we did not find a result in the literature at the level of generality required in this
article. For this reason, and for the sake of completeness, we include the proof of Lemma 3.7 in
Section A.6 of the appendix.

4
Green’s function representation
Recall the definition of the approximate density process uN in (2.8). In this section, we will
apply a technique used in [24,60] based on a Green’s function representation to obtain uniform
estimates on uN
k (t, x) for large k, and estimates on both space and time increments of (uN)N∈N.
We begin by briefly highlighting the main differences between our setting and that of [24,60].
Mueller and Tribe [60], and later Durrett and Fan [24], studied the density of particles in
a scaled version of the voter model. Since in their model, under a suitable rescaling, the local
density of particles is uniformly bounded by 1, a Green’s function representation combined with
random walk estimates allows them to prove Hölder estimates on the expectation of the particle
density. In our case, we have two main challenges. First, the particle density is not uniformly
bounded, so although we will be able to derive estimates for the space and time increments, we
will not be able to establish Hölder estimates for these increments. Second, since we must keep
track of infinitely many types of particles, we will prove estimates that depend on the subset of
types of particles considered (here, the ‘type’ of a particle refers to the number of mutations that
it carries). This will provide us with some control over uN
k (t, x) for large k. We will repeatedly
use the random walk estimates stated in Lemma A.3 in Section A.2 of the appendix.

Since the action of the reaction part of the generator of our process involves polynomials
in the local number of particles (recall (2.7)), we will need a weak equicontinuity property for
moments of the local density. This will be our next result.

following conditions:
(i) F separates points in M (R)N0, i.e. for any u, v ∈M (R)N0 with u ̸= v, there exists φ ∈F

Recall from the end of Section 1 that we equip M (R) with the vague topology. By combining
Lemma 5.6 and the characterisation of compact subsets of M (R) in the vague topology, we will
now be able to prove Proposition 5.2.
Proof of Proposition 5.2. Let (An)n∈N , (an)n∈N ⊂(0, ∞) be non-decreasing with limn→∞An =
∞. Let K ⊂M (R) be the subset of Radon measures given by
K := {ν ∈M (R) : ν([−An, An]) ≤an ∀n ∈N} .
Then K is a compact subset of M (R) in the vague topology (see [16, Section III.9 - Proposition
15]). Furthermore, recalling (2.10), since (M (R)N0, d) has the product topology, by Tychonoff’s
theorem (see for instance [33, Theorem 4.42]), if (Kk)k∈N0 is a sequence of compact subsets of
M (R), then the Cartesian product K := Q
k∈N0 Kk is a compact subset of M (R)N0.

We are now ready to prove Proposition 5.3.
Proof of Proposition 5.3. We checked before (5.20) that F satisfies conditions (i) and (ii). By
the tightness criterion stated in [29, Theorem 3.8.6] and in [1], the result follows directly from
Lemmas 5.7 and 5.8.
We can finally prove Proposition 5.1.
Proof of Proposition 5.1. By Jakubowski’s tightness criterion [44, Theorem 3.1], since the metric
space (M (R)N0, d) is complete and separable (see e.g. the comment before Proposition 3.4.6
in [29] for a discussion on the product topology), the result follows directly from Propositions 5.2
and 5.3.

We are now ready to prove Proposition 6.2.
Proof of Proposition 6.2. The desired tightness follows from Lemma 6.6, while condition (i)
follows from Lemma 6.7. To establish that v is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (2.11), we
first observe that since v ∈L4 deg q−([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1), since the heat kernel decays exponentially
fast in space and by the definition of the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 in (2.12), for any (t, x) ∈
[0, T] × R,

7
Uniqueness and regularity of mild solutions
Existence of λ-almost everywhere non-negative mild solutions to the system of PDEs (2.11) fol-
lows from Proposition 6.2. In this section, we will establish uniqueness and regularity properties
of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (2.11). For T > 0 and r ∈[1, ∞), recall the functional
space Lr([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1) defined in (6.2). For v(1), v(2) ∈Lr([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1), we call v(2) a
version of v(1) in Lr([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1) if
v(1)(t, x) = v(2)(t, x) for λ-almost every (t, x) ∈[0, T] × R,
(7.1)
where λ denotes the Lebesgue measure on R2. Recall the definition of mild solutions to the
system of PDEs (2.11) in Definition 3.5. Our first main result of this section concerns uniqueness
of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (2.11).
Proposition 7.1. Suppose m > 0, µ ∈[0, 1], (sk)k∈N0 satisfies Assumption 2, q+, q−: [0, ∞) →
[0, ∞) satisfy Assumption 3, and f ∈L∞(R; ℓ1) satisfies Assumption 4. For T > 0, let v(1), v(2) ∈
L4 deg q−([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1) be λ-almost everywhere non-negative mild solutions to the system of
PDEs (2.11).
Then v(2) is a version of v(1) in L4 deg q−([0, T] × R, ˆλ; ℓ1), i.e. v(1) and v(2)
satisfy (7.1).
63


## Page 64


We will also establish the following regularity property of mild solutions.
Proposition 7.2. Under the assumptions of Proposition 7.1, there exists a unique function
v = (vk)k∈N0 : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ+
1 such that v ∈C ((0, ∞) × R; ℓ+
1 ) and satisfies the following
conditions:
(i) v = (vk)k∈N0 is a global non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (2.11) in the
sense of Definition 3.5, and it satisfies (3.5) for all (t, x) ∈[0, ∞) × R, i.e. for any k ∈N0
and (t, x) ∈[0, ∞) × R,
vk(t, x) = (Ptfk)(x) +

Moreover, the unique function v = (vk)k∈N0 : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ+

We can now complete the proof of Theorem 2.1, the functional law of large numbers for the
spatial Muller’s ratchet.

topology on D

8
Asymptotic behaviour of the system of PDEs
In this section, we will investigate the asymptotic behaviour of solutions of the system of
PDEs (2.11), and prove Theorem 2.5, as well as Theorems 2.7, 2.8 and 2.9. In what follows, we
denote the version of the mild solution of the system of PDEs (2.11) given in Proposition 7.2 by
u = (uk(t, x))k∈N0, t≥0, x∈R.
We will refer to this solution as the continuous mild solution of (2.11).
We will assume that the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 defined in (2.12) is monostable, i.e. that
F = (Fk)k∈N0 satisfies conditions (i)-(v) of Definition 2.3, and that the initial condition f =
(fk)k∈N0 of the system of PDEs (2.11) satisfies both Assumptions 4 and 5. Our strategy to
determine the long-term behaviour of u will be to represent each of the functions uk : [0, T] ×
R →R in terms of a Feynman-Kac formula. We divide the reminder of this section into four
subsections. In Section 8.1, we state and prove the Feynman-Kac representation. We prove
Theorem 2.5 and Theorem 2.8 in Section 8.2. The control over the evolution of the prevalence
of mutations gained in Section 8.2, together with ideas adapted from [64], will be used to prove
Theorem 2.7 in Section 8.3. Finally, we will prove Theorem 2.9 in Section 8.4.
8.1
Feynman-Kac representation formula
Fix m ∈(0, ∞), let (W(t))t≥0 denote a Brownian motion run at speed m, and for every x ∈R,
let Px denote the probability measure associated to (W(t))t≥0 started from x, and let Ex denote
the corresponding expectation. In this section, we will extensively use a Feynman-Kac formula.
For ease of reference, we quote a version stated and proved in [10, Proposition 3.1], which does
not require continuity of the solution at time 0.

8.3
Spreading speed under Fisher-KPP dynamics
In this subsection, we will prove Theorem 2.7 by computing the spreading speed of the solution
of (2.11) when the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 satisfies a Fisher-KPP condition. Throughout
this subsection, we let u = (uk)k∈N0 : [0, ∞) × R →ℓ+
1 denote the continuous mild solution to
the system of PDEs (2.11) given in Proposition 7.2. We suppose that (sk)k∈N0, q+, q−and f
satisfy Assumptions 2, 3, 4 and 5, and that the reaction term F = (Fk)k∈N0 defined in (2.12) is
monostable (in the sense of Definition 2.3) and is of Fisher-KPP type, i.e. we suppose that (2.14)
holds.
Our strategy will be to adapt the arguments used in the proof of [64, Theorem 1.1]. We start
by proving a Hölder estimate for u0 that holds uniformly in time, analogous to [64, Lemma 2.2].

A.1
Stochastic chain rule
In this subsection, we will state a general stochastic chain rule formula.
In what follows,
let (S, dS) be some complete and separable metric space, let A ⊆C (S; R) be some vector
subspace of real-valued continuous functions defined on S, let L

unbounded) operator and let (η(t))t≥0 be an S-valued càdlàg process with initial distribution ν,
which is strongly Markovian with respect to its right-continuous natural filtration {Fη
t+}t≥0. We
will assume that L is the infinitesimal generator of (η(t))t≥0, and that (η(t))t≥0 is a solution of
the martingale problem for (L, A, ν), i.e. for any ϕ ∈A, the process (Mϕ(t))t≥0 given by, for all
T ≥0,

We begin by stating a general chain rule formula for Markov processes whose transition rates
are not almost surely bounded. This result is classical for Markov processes with uniformly
bounded transition rates. We prove the version that we state here in the companion article [56,
Lemma A.8].

Proof of Lemma 6.3. It will suffice to establish that K = K (C1, C2, (kh)h∈N, (Dh)h∈N, β, J, T)

Proof of Proposition 8.12. Let C = C(q+, q−) > 0 be such that estimate (A.58) from Lemma A.12

pleteness of Cb((0, T ∗]; L∞(R; ℓ1)), we can apply Banach’s fixed-point theorem (see e.g. [18,
Theorem 5.7]) to conclude that there exists a unique u∗∈Cb((0, T ∗]; L∞(R; ℓ1)) such that
(H(0, T ∗, f∗, t)u∗)(·) = u∗(t, ·) for all t ∈(0, T ∗].

estimate (A.58) from Lemma A.12 does not depend on the particular time interval), we can
define u∗globally in time, i.e. there is a unique u∗: (0, ∞) × R →ℓ1 such that for all n ∈N0,
